**⚠ Like stage 8, this row cannot be written before PR C lands — and for a sharper version of the same reason.** `error_analysis.py:63-83` imports from **three stage entry points at once** (`calibrate.py`, `evaluate.py`, `threshold.py`), more than any other module in the DAG. Declared honestly pre-extraction, this stage would dep on three sibling entry points and inherit every edge they carry; declared as the table originally had it — the entry module plus `explain.py` — it under-declares by three modules. Post-extraction the imports resolve to `artifacts.py` (`load_fitted_model`, `load_dev_oof_predictions`, and `load_dev_oof_diagnostics` once B0 repoints the carry-through) and `policy_config.py` (the `configs/policy/threshold.yaml` read at `:1193`), which is what makes the block above both honest and narrow.

**Deps — one addition, one deletion, and one absence that is an invariant rather than an omission.**
- **`models/shap_values.py` is declared and does not exist yet** — B2 carves it out so the pure V3 pieces stop living in this module. It is a dep because this stage still *calls* V3, in its reported-only form (`direction_sanity_check_test`): the binding copy moved pre-seal into `threshold`, but a change to the direction logic still changes what this stage reports.
- **🔴 `reports/metrics.json` is absent, and its removal is the settled outcome of B0's carry-through fix.** The table row already records this: `_load_dev_oof_diagnostics_carrythrough` dug four flag lists out of 42 KB of everything `evaluate` computed, so editing `test_ece` — a pure diagnostic — would have rerun the full SHAP pass, eleven figures and the cohort scan. B0 repoints the read at `load_dev_oof_diagnostics(run_id, cfg)`, i.e. `runs:/<run_id>/threshold/dev_oof_diagnostics.json`, which is same-cycle by construction. **The edge was never the right one anyway** — those flags originate in `threshold`, which already emits `dev_oof_diagnostics.json` as a `metrics:` out. A dep that would have been over-broad *and* pointed at the wrong producer is one the DAG is better without.
- **📌 `datasets/processed/split_manifest.parquet` is absent, and the absence is corroborating evidence — not enforcement.** Stage 8 declares it precisely because it partitions the sealed test set; this stage does not, and *must* not. It reaches sealed-test rows only through `reports/test_predictions.parquet`, joined to features by `customerid`, so no invalidation is lost: a re-split reruns `split` → `calibrate` → `evaluate`, and the new `test_predictions.parquet` reruns this stage transitively. Contrast with stage 8's 📌 note, which reads the same edge in the opposite direction.
  - **⚠ An earlier draft claimed *"the invariant is visible in the DAG rather than only in a test."* That inverts the two.** DVC never verifies that a stage read **only** what it declared — the deps-side analogue of the rule established in the params exemption list (*"DVC checks that a declared out exists and hashes it; it never verifies the stage authored it"*). A *declared* manifest would be a loud regression signal in review; an **undeclared** read of it is invisible to DVC entirely. So the DAG corroborates and the structural guard enforces. Getting this backwards matters because it makes the guard look redundant at exactly the moment it is carrying the invariant alone.
  - **🔴 And the guard as written is one hop short — today, not hypothetically.** Phase 7's structural check is `grep -r "data.split" src/telco_churn/models/error_analysis.py …`, which returns nothing and passes. But `error_analysis.py:67` imports from `evaluate.py`, and `evaluate.py:41` is `from telco_churn.data.split import partition`, with `_load_test_partition()` at `:183`. **`from telco_churn.models.evaluate import _load_test_partition` would pass that grep cleanly.** The invariant currently holds by behaviour — the three imported names do not partition — not by the guard. `evaluate.py:188-189`'s own docstring asserts *"no other module imports `data.split` for the test partition"*, which is textually true and one attribute access from false. **The same hole applies to the guard's other two roots**, `explain.py` and notebook 05; the fix is scoped to all three, not to stage 9 alone.
  - **The replacement is transitive import reachability, and it is a Phase 8 item gated on PR C.** An AST check in `test_architecture.py`: from each of the three roots, walk first-party imports transitively and assert `data.split` is unreachable. **Two things make this more than a better `grep`.** First, a flat one-level check over the modules this row declares would repeat the very defect it fixes, one hop further out — reachability has to be transitive or it is the same bug. Second, **it fails today by construction** (`error_analysis.py` → `evaluate.py` → `data.split`), so it cannot be written before the extraction: PR C is what makes the project's most-stated invariant statically checkable for the first time, which is a benefit of the extraction nothing else records.
  - **⚠ The brittleness to write down now, because it is the call someone gets wrong under time pressure.** The invariant is not *"only `evaluate.py` may import `data.split`"* — **three modules import it and two do so legitimately:** `calibrate.py:39` and `threshold.py` take the **dev** half (`dev_df, _test_df = partition(df)`), `evaluate.py:41` takes the **test** half. Which element of the tuple you take is what matters, and no static check can see it, so any guard here is a **layering proxy, not a proof**. The consequence: `artifacts.py` is shared by stages 6–9, and the day someone moves a dev-partition helper into it for `calibrate`'s benefit, stage 9's reachability check fires and the failure looks spurious — the dev half is genuinely allowed. **The correct response is to split the module, never to relax the root set**, because the check would be reporting a real layering violation: stage 9 would again be one import from the partitioner. A guard relaxed the first time it fires was never a guard.
  - **Cost/benefit, since this section demands it of its other guards: this one earns more than the correspondence guard beside it.** That one defends against a repoint nobody is likely to make in a project with a static dataset. This one closes a live hole in the invariant `CLAUDE.md` states first and most often — reachable today, by a single import, past every check currently in place.
- **`features/accessor.py` and the features parquet are in** — `load_features` (`:60`) supplies the feature values every SHAP attribution, cohort scan and value-decile profile is computed over. `features/build.py` stays out on the `TARGET_COL` reasoning stages 6 and 8 both use: the constant is materialised as a column in the parquet already declared, and a mismatch raises `KeyError` rather than going stale.

**Params — one file, and three keys that each come out for a different reason.** The table row declared `configs/config.yaml: random_seed` plus the error-analysis config; only the second survives.
- **`random_seed` is a phantom, and this is the sixth consecutive stage to carry it.** A full enumeration of `cfg.` reads in `error_analysis.py` returns no seed of any kind — not `cfg.random_seed`, not a step-level `random_state`. The module is deterministic given its inputs; the SHAP explainer is seeded by the fitted model it loads. The declaration would have invalidated the DAG's most expensive analytical stage on a key it never reads.
- **🔴 `mlflow.registered_model_name` comes out, and B1 is what removes it — this is the one param whose status *changes* rather than being corrected.** Today `error_analysis.py:1177` reads it, and the read has exactly two consumers: the model-version tag write at `:1809`, and `load_fitted_model`'s registry URI for the explicit-version path. **B1 moves the tag write into `register.py`**, which deletes the first. The second survives, but `dvc repro` never takes that path — B0 resolves the model from `reports/calibrate_receipt.json`'s `model_uri`, and the registry URI is only built when a human passes `error_analysis.model_version=N`. So under every invocation the DAG makes, the key is unread. **Declared, it would be a phantom that a code reader could not identify as one**, because the read is genuinely there in the module — just on a branch `dvc repro` cannot enter. Stage 8 keeps its own declaration of this key, and correctly: there the comparative regime's champion lookup reads it on the repro path.
- **`paths.reports` / `paths.figures` and `mlflow.experiment_name` are all read and all exempt** — the first two under the `paths.*` rule, the third as a record-location key. Worth noting together because this stage is the clearest case for the exemption list's own L1 warning: `paths.reports` and `paths.figures` appear in the module's `cfg.` reads, while `experiment_name` does **not** — it is read inside `ensure_experiment_metadata`, in a `utils/` module this stage does not dep on. A key-granular pass over this row would see two of the three.
- **`configs/error_analysis/default.yaml` is declared whole**, and the row is where the `top_k_shap_features` → `top_k_dependence_features` rename lands: post-B2 the key drives only the dependence grid and the `shap_importance_*` metrics, the binding cutoff having moved to `configs/threshold/default.yaml`. **Whole-file is right here despite the aliasing trap**, not in spite of it: the module reads the sub-config both as `cfg.error_analysis.top_k_shap_features` and — inside helpers that receive the sub-tree as `cfg` — as a bare `cfg.top_k_shap_features`, along with seven more bare keys. A key-granular declaration would have to track which helper aliases what, which is exactly the maintenance burden whole-file declaration avoids when a file is small and entirely consumed by one stage.

**Outs — the deferred `error_analysis.json` question, resolved the same way stage 8 resolved `metrics.json`.** The table row has `reports/error_analysis.json` as the stage's only out. It does not survive the three-clause test.
- **Clause (a) — propagate staleness downstream — cannot be met by anything this stage produces.** `error_analysis` is the terminal stage; `register.py` is deliberately *not* a DVC stage, because it mutates shared pointer-based registry state. There is no downstream consumer inside the DAG for an out to invalidate.
- **Clause (b) is met by the wrong artifact.** `CLAUDE.md` requires `register.py` to resolve `error_analysis.json` via `runs:/<error_analysis_run_id>/error_analysis.json` and forbids reading it from `reports/` — *"a fixed local path reflects whichever run last executed `error_analysis.py` on this machine."* So the one consumer that exists is contractually barred from reading the local copy. **An out whose only reader may not read it is a mirror with a hash attached.**
- **Clause (c) argues for the receipt instead.** `reports/error_analysis_receipt.json` — the `{run_id, logged_model_id}` shape — is small, flat, diffable, and is `register.py`'s actual channel to this run once B1 stops the tag write. It is `cache: false` so it lands in git, which also keeps the stage clean on a fresh clone: DVC finds the git-tracked receipt at its recorded hash and does not rerun the SHAP pass.
- **`figures/` and `shap_values.parquet` stay out for reasons already settled** — the first because four stages share `reports/figures/` and two stages naming one directory out is a parse-time DVC error; the second because `CLAUDE.md` requires the SHAP array to be *logged*, and MLflow is where a cross-cycle comparison of it is actually made.
- **⚠ State the trade rather than discovering it later: this is the stage where receipt-mediated edges cost the most.** Everything expensive here — the SHAP values, the cohort scan, eleven figures — now lives only in MLflow, which DVC cannot restore. `dvc checkout` on an old commit brings back the receipt, not the analysis. That is the right trade (MLflow is where these are compared across cycles, and a 1,409-row SHAP matrix is not a git artifact), but it means **the MLflow store is part of this pipeline's recovery surface, not an optional viewer** — which is why the tracking backend rule in `CLAUDE.md` is a correctness constraint and not a preference. **→ This stage is where the trade is largest, but the property is DAG-wide and is recorded as the reproducibility-contract item in the DAG-wide corrections** — including the precise failure state (DVC cache intact, MLflow gone, `dvc status` clean), the receipt-resolvability check that detects it, and the runbook entry for recovering from it. Do not treat it as a stage 9 caveat.
- **Not adding a `metrics:` entry, recorded so the omission is not later read as an oversight.** The tempting one is a flat fairness-flag summary for cross-cycle diffing. `dvc metrics diff` compares **scalars**, and V1/V2/V2b flags are lists of feature and segment names, so the tool would render them poorly at best. The cross-cycle comparison this stage genuinely needs is the one Phase 10 already requires — each cycle's runs as comparable peers in the MLflow UI — and V3 is reported-only here post-B2, so no scalar on this stage gates anything.

**`cmd` passes no Hydra override**, on stages 6–8's both-or-neither rule: `configs/error_analysis/default.yaml` declares `model_version: null`, `dvc repro` supplies nothing, and `__main__` reads `reports/calibrate_receipt.json` for `{run_id, logged_model_id, model_uri}`. **One consequence specific to this stage:** `error_analysis.py:2006-2011` currently *raises* when `cfg.error_analysis.model_version` is None, which is the pre-B0 contract — under `dvc repro` that is the normal case, so B0's change here is not merely additive plumbing but the difference between the stage running and aborting on its first invocation.

---

### Phase 9 — FastAPI Serving + Streamlit UI *(3 days)*

**What this achieves:** The champion model is accessible via a REST API with a health endpoint, batch prediction support, and built-in Prometheus metrics. A Streamlit UI lets a non-technical user pull up an existing customer by ID and see the churn probability alongside the top reasons, or manually edit a profile for a what-if scenario or a prospective customer with no account yet — both paths call the same API.

**Deliverables:**
- **Capacity-constrained contact policy (`top-K by expected value`) — resolved here, not in Phase 7.** Phase 6's `threshold.py` flags it: *"when the implied contact rate exceeds capacity, the correct policy is not a higher threshold but top-K by expected value `p·r·LTV − c`."* Raising `t*` until the flagged count fits the retention team's capacity abandons the Bayes-optimal cut for an arbitrary one; ranking by EV and taking as many as the budget allows is the correct response. **This is a serving-time decision mechanism, which is why it lives here** — the evaluation layer has no knowledge of operational capacity and must not invent one. Compare `threshold.json`'s `implied_contact_rate` against the configured capacity; when it fits, ship the fixed `t*` unchanged; when it does not, generate the contact list by top-K EV.
  > **Under the current cost model this is rank-identical to top-K-by-score, and that is expected — not a bug.** `c`, `r`, and `LTV` are scenario-level constants (`ANALYSIS.md` §0), so `EV_i = p_i · (r × LTV) − c` is a strictly increasing function of `p_i` and preserves rank order exactly. So implement the *policy* (the capacity constraint is real and the K-cut is real) but do not expect a different customer ordering than the score ranking gives. The two diverge only under a **per-customer `LTV`** — recorded in §0 as the leading v2 item, and the change that would make this mechanism earn its name.
- `src/telco_churn/serving/schemas.py` — Pydantic v2 request/response models; field constraints aligned with the Pandera schema (single source of truth)
- `src/telco_churn/serving/predict.py` — loads the `champion` model and preprocessor from MLflow at startup; exposes `predict_single` and `predict_batch`
  - **This phase does not depend on Phase 10 (Prefect) existing — the champion it loads was already promoted in Phase 7, with zero Prefect involvement.** Registration is performed by `register.py` (Phase 7, extended in Phase 8's boundary revisit), invoked via a standalone CLI that already works manually and already minted this project's first champion. Prefect's only role, starting Phase 10, is a `pause_flow_run`/notify/resume wrapper *around* that same CLI for the unattended weekly retrain cycle — it does not perform registration itself, so nothing about a champion existing in the registry requires an orchestrator to be running. `predict.py` resolving `models:/telco-churn-pipeline@champion` at startup only needs MLflow's registry (Postgres-backed) to already hold a promoted version, which has been true since Phase 7.
    > Containerising this phase doesn't change that, and if anything makes the boundary more visible rather than less: the `api`/`ui` services this phase adds to `docker-compose.yml` need only the already-running `infra` profile (Postgres + MLflow, up since Phase 1/5) to reach the registry. Prefect gets no service in `docker-compose.yml` until Phase 10 adds one — there is no shared container, network dependency, or startup-ordering requirement between the two.
    > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — how does a champion promoted by Prefect (Phase 10) actually reach an already-running production container?** `predict.py` loads the champion once, at startup. A promotion is just an MLflow alias flip — Prefect's registry write and the live container's in-memory model are two independent things, and nothing here connects them yet. `cd.yml` (Phase 11) doesn't help by default: it triggers on a **code push to `main`**, and a model promotion is not a commit — it can happen on a schedule or a drift signal with zero code changes involved. Two candidate resolutions, neither chosen:
    > 1. **Hot-reload inside the serving app.** Extend `resolve_serving_model()` (already planned above, for the shadow/canary seam) to periodically re-check which version `champion` points to — a cached lookup with a short TTL, or a background poll — and reload the in-memory model on change. No restart, no deploy, no cross-system trigger: the registry is the single source of truth, Prefect writes to it, the API independently reads from it on its own cadence. Reuses machinery this phase already commits to building, rather than adding new coupling.
    > 2. **Prefect triggers a redeploy after promotion.** `register.py`'s promotion step, once it flips `champion`, restarts the App Runner service — either a direct AWS API call, or by firing a GitHub Actions `workflow_dispatch` so the restart is visible in the same place code deploys are (Phase 11's `cd.yml`). Gives an auditable trail of model promotions alongside code deploys, a legitimate portfolio-demonstration reason to prefer it — at the cost of Prefect needing AWS or GitHub credentials, and `cd.yml` needing a second, non-push-triggered entry point.
    >
    > Resolve before Phase 10's `training_cycle.py` is implemented — a promotion that never reaches production makes the whole retrain cycle a no-op from the serving side.
  - **⚠ Leave the shadow/canary seam, even though no live traffic ever flows through it.** Real deployments do not point production at a new model on an offline gate alone — a challenger scores live traffic in **shadow** (logged, not served) or a **canary** slice before the pointer moves (`ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13). This project has no feed, so none of that can *run* — but the serving layer must be *structured* so it could: resolve the served model through a single indirection (a `resolve_serving_model()` that returns `champion` today) rather than hard-coding `models:/…@champion` at the call site, so a `challenger` shadow route plugs in without touching the request path. **Do not synthesize traffic to make a shadow comparison appear to execute** — a canary whose evidence you authored is a prop, not a demonstration, and it breaks the same honesty line as the drift stack (build the machinery, be explicit about what has never fired). The seam is the deliverable; the traffic is not.
  - **Serving-parity test against Phase 6's golden fixture.** `golden_predictions.json` (written by **`calibrate.py`** from the in-memory fitted pipeline, logged on the registered run, mirrored to `reports/`, and already verified through the `champion` alias by `register.py`) holds a row sample pinned by `customerID` and the scores the champion produced for them at promotion time. Assert the API returns those same scores through the full request path — Pydantic parse → `ColumnTransformer` → `predict_proba`. This is what catches a serving-layer bug that silently changes predictions (a column reordered, a dtype coerced, a category mapped to the wrong level) — the class of failure that produces plausible numbers and no error, and would otherwise be invisible until someone reconciled the API against the offline metrics. Phase 11's CI runs the same assertion.
  - **⚠ The `Dockerfile` must build from the champion's own locked environment, not from `pyproject.toml`'s open version ranges — this is the precondition the golden-parity tolerance above (and Phase 7's) actually rests on.** `register.py`'s smoke check compares reloaded scores against `golden_predictions.json` at `atol=1e-9`, and that tolerance is only well-posed if the scoring environment never drifts from the one `calibrate.py` logged the model under: a `numpy`/`scikit-learn`/`lightgbm` point-release bump shifts floating-point output in the same 1e-7–1e-9 range as the tolerance itself, with no actual bug present. `register.py` enforces this at the moment of promotion (comparing the registered version's own logged `requirements.txt`, via `mlflow.pyfunc.get_model_dependencies()`, against the environment running the check) — but that check only covers registration, not every later container build. So the serving image must install from **`uv.lock`** at the commit the champion was trained under, or — more robust to a stale lock — from the champion's own MLflow-logged `requirements.txt`/`conda.yaml`, fetched at build time; never from `pyproject.toml`'s ranges resolved fresh at image-build time. A container built that way, run months after promotion, would fail this same test for an environment reason that presents as a serialization bug, sending the reader to debug the wrong layer. Phase 11's CD pipeline builds this image exactly once per merge and redeploys the same SHA to staging and production, so getting the base image right here is a one-time discipline, not a per-deploy one.
- **Threshold as policy, not model state:** `/predict` returns the **calibrated `P(churn)`**; the decision rule (operating threshold, any per-segment cuts, EV formula) lives in a **separate versioned config / policy layer** loaded at startup, changeable without redeploying the model artifact. This keeps the business-owned operating point decoupled from the model — see `summary.md` §4.5. The response includes both the probability and the decision so callers can apply their own threshold if they prefer.
- `src/telco_churn/serving/app.py` — FastAPI app:
  - `POST /predict` — single customer prediction
  - `POST /predict/batch` — batch scoring (array of `CustomerFeatures` objects); same model and preprocessor as the single endpoint — batch is a delivery mode, not a model change; the prediction unit (one customer per score) is identical in both modes
  - `GET /customer/{customerid}` — read-only lookup of an existing customer's raw feature values from Postgres (`customerid` is already the Phase 1 primary key), used to prefill the UI form instead of requiring all fields typed from scratch; not a model endpoint, no write path
  - `GET /health` — liveness probe
  - `GET /ready` — readiness probe (model loaded)
  - `GET /metrics` — Prometheus metrics via `prometheus_fastapi_instrumentator`
  - Structured log per prediction: request ID, features, probability, threshold, decision

> **Note — batch as the operational backbone:** In production, batch is typically the primary delivery mode. A nightly/weekly job scores the entire active customer base, writes results to a `churn_scores` table, and the CRM reads from there. Real-time (`/predict`) is the supplement — used for event-triggered interventions (e.g., a customer calls to cancel). The `pipelines/batch_predict.py` flow in Phase 10 is the scheduled incarnation of this pattern. The prediction unit (`a single customer per score`) is identical in both modes.

- `src/telco_churn/ui/streamlit_app.py` — two entry modes over the same 19 raw-input fields (`build_feature_df` derives the 20th, `charge_per_service`, automatically — nothing to enter for it): **(1) Lookup** — enter a `customerid`, `GET /customer/{customerid}` prefills every field, review and predict. This is the default tab and the realistic operational flow: a retention agent is working an existing account, not fabricating one field-by-field. **(2) Manual / what-if** — fields are directly editable, blank for a prospective customer with no DB record, or pre-filled from a lookup and then tweaked to test a scenario (e.g. "if we cut `monthlycharges` by $10 as a retention offer, does the probability drop enough to justify it?"). Both modes → `POST /predict` → probability gauge + top-5 SHAP contributions.
- `Dockerfile` (multi-stage, FastAPI) + `Dockerfile.ui` (Streamlit); both added to `docker-compose.yml`
- `tests/integration/test_api.py` — FastAPI test client: `/predict` returns valid schema; `/health` returns 200; batch endpoint accepts arrays; `/customer/{customerid}` returns 404 for an unknown ID

> **Note — lookup-first is a serving-layer fix, not a feature-selection one.** A blank 20-field form is the wrong default for a churn tool: real users work existing accounts, they don't manually reconstruct a customer profile from memory. Solving that friction by shrinking the model's input space would have meant re-litigating the §3/§5 `03b-feature-selection.ipynb` decision — where the paired-bootstrap test materially favoured the full 20-feature set (Δ = 0.0173, CI [0.0104, 0.0246]) — against a second, competing objective (form usability), which the project's single-metric selection invariant (`CLAUDE.md`) explicitly rules out. Fixing it here instead, at the UI/serving layer, keeps the frozen feature set untouched and leaves manual entry available for the cases that actually need it (what-if scenarios, prospective customers).

> **❓ OPEN QUESTION — where does `model_card.json` actually get shown to a stakeholder?** `CLAUDE.md` calls `model_card.json` (Phase 7 `register.py`) the model's "stakeholder-facing narrative," but no phase in this plan renders it anywhere a stakeholder would actually look — today it is an MLflow artifact on the promoted run, mirrored to `reports/`, readable only by opening the JSON directly. Candidate resolutions, none yet chosen:
>
> 1. **A third Streamlit tab** (e.g. "About this model") that fetches `model_card.json` via the `champion`-aliased run and renders it as readable sections — consistent with this phase's existing lookup/manual tabs, and the cheapest place to add it since the Streamlit app already exists here.
> 2. **A static rendered doc regenerated at promotion** (e.g. `reports/model_card.md`, templated from the JSON) — no new UI surface, but no live "current champion" view either; goes stale after a rollback unless regenerated on every alias flip too.
> 3. **Leave it as a JSON artifact, by design.** Treat "stakeholder-facing" as describing the *format* (structured, no raw hyperparameters/hashes, plain-language fields) rather than committing to a rendered surface, and defer actual display to whatever future consumer needs it.
>
> Resolve before this phase's Streamlit app is scoped in detail — cheap to add as a third tab if decided early, awkward to bolt on after the two-mode UI is already built and tested.

**Verification:** `docker compose up && curl -X POST http://localhost:8000/predict -d @example_payload.json` returns a churn probability; `curl http://localhost:8000/customer/<id>` returns that customer's raw fields; Streamlit at `:8501` displays a prediction with SHAP contributions in both lookup and manual mode.

---

### Phase 10 — Prefect Orchestration (Continuous Training) *(2–3 days)*

**What this achieves:** The model retrains automatically every week and checks for data drift every day — without manual intervention. The Prefect UI provides a full audit trail of every run, including failures.

**Deliverables:**
- Prefect 3 server added to `docker-compose.yml` (UI at `:4200`)
- `pipelines/training_cycle.py` — **named for the cycle, not for the steady state; this was `retrain.py` in earlier drafts and the rename is deliberate.** The flow is regime-general: the same steps run whether or not a `champion` exists, and this phase's own notes below depend on that — cold start forces `require_review: true` *inside this flow*, and the "three separate axes" note states outright that there is one escalation path gated differently, "not a cold-start path and a separate retrain path." A file named `retrain.py` asserts an incumbent always exists, which those notes deny; `training_cycle` is also already this project's term of art for exactly this span (`CLAUDE.md`: *"one registration per training cycle"*). This project's own cold start happened manually in Phase 7, so the flow may never take that branch here — but a fresh Phase 12 environment or a disaster-recovery rebuild would, and no code exists yet, so the rename is free now and never again. Prose that says "the weekly retrain" stays accurate: retraining is what the steady state does, not what the file is limited to. **Ripple:** `CLAUDE.md`'s `pipelines/` structure comment lists the flows as `(retrain, drift_check, batch_predict)` — update it in the same PR that creates the directory. Weekly Sunday 02:00 schedule; runs `ingest → validate → features → train → calibrate → evaluate → register`, with a Prefect suspend/resume boundary between `evaluate` and `register` for the human review step (mechanism specified in the dedicated note immediately below); promotes `challenger` to `champion` via `register.py`'s gate — the same gate, in its **comparative regime** (`ANALYSIS.md` §0): PR-AUC must improve on the incumbent champion (selection), while recall@`t*` ≥ 0.65 and Brier non-inferiority hold as vetoes. Phase 7 exercised the same gate's cold-start regime; nothing is re-specified here. As in Phase 7, `calibrate.py` performs the cycle's only registration and `register.py` mints nothing new — a pass simply flips `champion` onto the same version `challenger` already points at, logged as `model_promoted`. Re-calibrated with the **pinned method** (`calibration.method` from config — never `auto`, which would let the method flip on Brier noise between weekly cycles). Inherits the operating threshold rather than re-deriving it (the closed-form cut is a function of costs, not of the model). **Dummy-floor guardrail:** each cycle recomputes the prevalence / `DummyClassifier(strategy='prior')` floor on the current data and asserts the retrained model clears it by a margin — a near-dummy result signals a broken feature/label pipeline (stale join, dropped column), not a modelling regression, and blocks promotion with a structured alarm. The floor is recomputed each cycle, never hardcoded, because churn prevalence drifts.
  > **📌 Review → register handoff, concretely — this is the mechanism; whether it fires every cycle is a separate, still-open policy question (see the `require_review` open question further below).** Phase 8's PR B1 built `review.py` (`run_review_step`, wrapping `gate.py::record_review`) as a standalone CLI precisely so this phase would have something to call instead of a notebook cell. Concretely: `training_cycle.py` runs the automated chain through `evaluate.py`/`error_analysis.py`, then calls `pause_flow_run` (a Prefect `Suspend`) at the point `register.py` would otherwise run, and fires a notification linking to that cycle's already-written `promotion_decision.json`/`error_analysis.json` — nothing further to render, they're files. A human resumes the flow by submitting a verdict through Prefect's typed resume input; the resume handler invokes `review.py`'s `run_review_step` with that verdict, then immediately runs `register.py`'s mint/flip/reject logic in the same resumed task. Same two scripts, same functions as the Phase 8 manual flow (`python -m telco_churn.models.review` then `python -m telco_churn.models.register`, run by hand) — only the trigger changes, from two separate terminal commands to one Prefect resume.
  > **📌 The alias flip is a distinct final step, behind a `require_traffic_validation` gate — because the weekly retrain is where the missing shadow/canary stage actually bites.** Phase 7's cold-start flip has no incumbent to shadow against; this one replaces a *serving* champion with a challenger, and in a real deployment that flip would follow a shadow or canary evaluation on live traffic, never an offline gate alone (`ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13). This dataset has no feed, so structure the flow to make the absence *legible and pluggable* rather than invisible: the promotion decision and the `set_registered_model_alias` call are **separate steps**, with a `require_traffic_validation` config gate between them that **no-ops on a static dataset** and carries a one-line comment marking it as the shadow/canary insertion point. **Do not fabricate traffic to make the gate appear to run** — a green light you wired to always-green is a prop, and it breaks the same honesty line as the demonstrative promotion gate two notes below. The seam is real; the traffic validation is explicitly stubbed, not faked.
  > **📌 ✅ Feature selection must not silently re-run every cycle — resolved upstream by Phase 8's selection retirement; no flag and no narrower path is needed.** The requirement stands and the reasoning is still load-bearing (the hyperparameter open question below borrows it wholesale): a borderline feature flipping in or out between cycles from split noise alone would confound tuning, which assumes a fixed input space, and make week-to-week model versions incomparable. What changed is that **it is now satisfied by construction rather than by a mechanism.** Earlier drafts of this note proposed giving `__main__.py` a flag to skip Steps 1–3 and reload the committed `feature_columns.txt`, or routing this flow through a re-tuning-only path. Both presumed a freeze that could move; Phase 8's prerequisites delete the CV wrapper's *automated-pipeline call site* and the `decision == "reduced"` branch, so `committed_features` is read from `features/schema.py::COMMITTED_FEATURES` — a hand-maintained constant distinct from `FEATURE_SCHEMA` (the full candidate space) — and the input space cannot drift between cycles no matter how the flow invokes `train`. Building either mechanism now would add a knob whose only job is to prevent something that can no longer happen. **Step 3 does still run every cycle** — the retained `mint_committed_list` fit, SHAP audit, and correlated-group joint permutation — but it is a *diagnostic feeding the model card and `error_analysis.py`*, not a gate, and re-running it per cycle is desirable: those importances should describe the model actually being promoted. **The one thing to preserve is the guarantee, not the note:** if selection is ever reintroduced, this requirement becomes live again and `_study_name()`'s `committed_features` component is what would surface the drift. *(An earlier version of this note cited `paymentmethod` at "49/100 fold stability" — that figure is one of the contested values in the Phase 8 QA checklist, where §4b's prose, §4b's own table, and run `de4fac8e`'s artifact give 49, 33, and 38. Dropped rather than corrected here; the number was illustrative and the argument never rested on it.)*
  > **📌 ✅ Model family selection must not silently re-run every cycle — resolved by the same freeze pattern, landed alongside Phase 8's selection retirement.** The adjacent axis to the note above, and it went unaddressed for longer: `models/train/__main__.py` re-ran the full Dummy/LogReg/LightGBM comparison and paired-bootstrap family decision (`candidates.py`, `comparison.py`) on every training cycle, so a scheduled retrain here would re-litigate "is LightGBM still better than LogReg" from scratch every week — the identical comparability/cost problem the feature-selection retirement above exists to prevent, for a decision `ANALYSIS.md` §4a already records as concluded (Δ = 0.007, CI [0.002, 0.012], reinforced by build-specific rationale — SHAP speed, calibration continuity, training speed). Resolved the same way: the automated call site is deleted, `candidates.py`/`comparison.py` stay fully tested in `src/` but run only from `03a-model-selection.ipynb`'s on-demand review, and the decision is frozen into **`models/train/common.py::COMMITTED_MODEL_FAMILY`** — a hand-maintained constant, edited only via reviewed PR. `training_manifest.json`'s `model_comparison` section (the recomputed paired-Δ) is replaced by a referenced `model_family_committed` section, since there is nothing left to recompute automatically.
  > **📌 The CV wrapper is not gone, and reopening the decision has a defined path — this is the mechanism the guarantee above would fall back to.** `features/select.py::run_selection_cv` + `reduced_set_bootstrap_test` are retained, fully tested, and callable from `notebooks/03b-feature-selection.ipynb`'s "On-demand ablation review" section — they are simply never called by `training_cycle.py` or any other automated entry point. **The correct trigger is a per-feature drift signal, not routine model drift.** Phase 13's Evidently check flags *which* features moved, feature by feature (PSI or equivalent) — that is the signal that the correlation structure the standing ablation measured may no longer hold, and it is what should prompt a human to open the notebook and re-run the review, not a generic "PR-AUC dropped this week" alert, which `training_cycle.py`'s own comparative-regime gate and recalibration already handle without touching the schema. The other two triggers are non-drift: a new feature adopted via `02a-feature-discovery.ipynb`, or a scheduled periodic review with no other cause. **The response is a code change, not a flow parameter.** If the review's conclusion changes, `COMMITTED_FEATURES` is edited directly (a normal reviewed PR) — `training_cycle.py` needs no awareness of *why* the constant changed, no flag, no branch; the next scheduled run picks up whatever `COMMITTED_FEATURES` says, same as any other code change shipped to `main`. The review logs to its own `telco-churn-feature-selection-review` MLflow experiment, kept separate from `telco-churn-training` so a manually-triggered review is never mistaken for one of this flow's own runs.
  > **📌 Persist the dev-OOF probability vector every cycle — do not rely on Phase 7's recomputation trick.** Phase 6's `calibrate.py` logs only `calibration_summary.json` and a figure: it persists its *conclusions* (method selected, BSS, `t*`) but not the **evidence they rest on**, discarding the calibrated OOF probability vector. Phase 7 works around this by recomputing the vector deterministically and writing `reports/dev_oof_predictions.parquet` — which is sound *only because the dataset is static and the folds are seeded*. **That workaround stops being reproducible the moment retraining is real.** Under a weekly cadence each cycle has its own data, its own folds, and its own calibration map, and "just recompute it" silently reconstructs a *different* vector than the one that cycle's decisions were made on. From this phase forward, `calibrate.py` should log the OOF vector as a first-class run artifact — alongside `calibration_summary.json`, on the same run — so every cycle's drift reference, veto surface, and calibration slope are computed from the numbers that cycle actually decided on. This is a one-line `log_artifact`, and it retires the workaround rather than inheriting it.
  > **📌 Warm-starting Optuna across retrain cycles.** Phase 5's `configs/tuning/optuna.yaml` `warm_start_params` is a one-time, hand-set prior (the archived notebook's own Optuna best) with no equivalent once that notebook is out of the loop. From this phase on, the `train` step should populate `warm_start_params` dynamically from the current `champion`-aliased MLflow run's logged params (`mlflow.get_run(champion_run_id).data.params`) before calling `run_tuning_step`, instead of reading a hardcoded config block — each retrain's winner becomes the next cycle's informed prior. `tuning.py`'s `study.enqueue_trial(...)` mechanism needs no change for this; only the source of the dict does.
  > - **This is also what pays for the study-key fix, and that is the more load-bearing reason to build it here.** Phase 8's prerequisites complete `_study_name()`'s cache key with a real `data_content_hash`, which is a correctness fix but costs the free rerun: any data change now mints a fresh study and pays a full cold search instead of resuming. Warm-starting is how that cost comes back **without** the correctness violation, and the distinction is exact — resumption reuses the previous study's *scores*, which is invalid once the data has changed (it mixes trials fitted on two datasets into one 1-SE selection pool); `enqueue_trial` reuses only its *parameter suggestions*, and every enqueued trial is re-fitted and re-scored on the current data. So each score in the new study comes from the new data, TPE starts in the incumbent's neighbourhood rather than cold, and a scheduled retrain can legitimately run fewer trials than a cold start.
  > - **⚠ `study.add_trials(prior.trials)` looks like the same thing and is the bug.** It imports the prior trials *with their original scores*, reconstituting exactly the mixed-dataset pool the completed cache key exists to prevent — and it does so inside a correctly-named fresh study, where nothing would flag it. `enqueue_trial` re-runs; `add_trials` imports stale numbers. Whichever resolution the hyperparameter open question below takes, this distinction holds.
  > **Clarification — what "rolling holdout" means here, since `customers_raw` doesn't grow.** The raw Kaggle CSV is a static, read-only snapshot (`CLAUDE.md`) — this project has no live customer feed appending new rows, so the DVC `split`/`ingest`/`validate` stages have no changed deps to react to, and `training_cycle.py`'s `ingest → validate → features` steps are cache hits every cycle. `data.split.make_split()` therefore runs once, ever, for this project; `split_manifest.parquet` is never regenerated, and there is no new-customer-driven reshuffling problem to solve. The "do not reuse the sealed test set — use a rolling/time-based holdout instead" guidance (§ Phase 7 evaluate.py, § Deliberate scope above) is about a different risk: scoring the *same* frozen `evaluate.py` test partition against every weekly promotion decision implicitly "spends" that sealed set dozens of times a year, eroding it as an unbiased estimate (repeated-peeking, not data-freshness). The data that genuinely grows over time in this system is `prediction_outcomes` (below, `performance_check.py`) — live predictions joined to realised labels as they mature — not `customers_raw`. Any future rolling-holdout construction for promotion decisions should be carved from `prediction_outcomes`/logged-prediction history, not from a re-partitioned `customers_raw`; the mechanism itself is not yet decided and is deferred to this phase's implementation.
  > - **⚠ A time-based cut is not available, and no split definition can create one.** IBM Telco is a **cross-sectional snapshot**: one row per customer, one moment, churn already labelled. The raw CSV has **no date column** — `tenure` is a *duration* (months as a customer), not a timestamp — so there is no time axis to cut along. `data.split.make_split()` is a random stratified `train_test_split` (`split.py:80`) not by preference but because it is the only split the data supports. This matters beyond terminology: in a real deployment the holdout is defined *temporally* and renews itself every cycle, which is precisely why "the test set is spent" is a non-problem in production. **That mechanism is unavailable here, and it is the dataset's shape — not the method — that forecloses it.** Any candidate resolution below that presumes a temporal cut is not viable; deterministic hashing over an existing partition is the only splitting mechanism this data admits.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — what does the weekly promotion gate actually score, on a static dataset?** `customers_raw` is a fixed, non-growing snapshot: there is no way to produce a genuinely fresh evaluation surface for each retrain cycle, and `CLAUDE.md`'s own invariant already forbids reusing the original sealed test set repeatedly for challenger-vs-champion comparisons — repeated peeking erodes it, even though nothing ever trains on it. So `register.py`'s gate in its comparative regime (`ANALYSIS.md` §0 — `challenger` improves on `champion`'s PR-AUC, subject to the recall and Brier vetoes) has, as of Phase 10, **no defined evaluation set**. Note this is a question about the gate's *input data*, not its *rule* — and the rule is **fully settled**, so this is the only piece left. §0 fixes what is measured, what each metric may do with the answer, and how uncertainty binds (paired bootstrap on Δ, materiality Δ\* = 0.005, CI excludes 0 in the challenger's favour); Phase 7's `register.py` and `utils/stats.py::paired_bootstrap_metric_ci` implement it, and Phase 7 already exercises the guardrails and the decision function under test. What §0 deliberately does not name is the *rows* those metrics are computed over once the sealed set is spent. That is this phase's decision to make, and it is all that stands between the comparative regime and going live. Three candidate resolutions, none yet chosen:
  >
  > 1. **Score on `prediction_outcomes`.** Correct in principle, and it is what a real deployment would do. **⚠ But on this dataset it is not merely delayed — it never arrives, and the plan previously described it as a cold-start problem, which is wrong.** `prediction_outcomes` is *"live predictions joined to realised labels as they mature"* — and there are no maturing labels: churn is already fixed in the frozen CSV, for every one of the 7,043 customers, all of whom the champion trained on. No observation window ever closes, because no outcome is ever pending. `ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13 states this outright (*"the realised-performance loop has no labels to mature"*); this bullet contradicted it and is corrected here. **Option 1 is viable for the design and dead for this instance** — retain it as the documented production answer, not as something Phase 10 can wait for.
  > 2. **Nested CV on the full dataset per cycle.** Statistically defensible on static data and available immediately, but it measures *the spec*, not *the artifact*, and it re-fits every cycle — expensive, and the resulting number is not comparable to the Phase 7 sealed-test metrics of record.
  > 3. **Declare the gate demonstrative.** On a fixed 7,043-row snapshot with no live feed, Phase 10's weekly retrain is a *mechanism demonstration* — the plumbing, alerting, and alias-flip are real; the statistical claim behind the promotion decision is not. Honest, cheap, and arguably the right answer for a portfolio project. **`ANALYSIS.md` §9 limitation #13 now states this outright** (for drift, for the realised-performance loop, and for this gate), which makes option 3 a matter of *accepting* a documented boundary rather than newly conceding one.
  >
  > **⚠ Once option 1 is seen to be dead on this dataset, the question is far less open than "three candidates" suggests — and the honest reading is that option 3 is forced.** Option 1 never arrives (no labels mature). Option 2 is available but measures *the spec*, not *the artifact*, and yields a number not comparable to the Phase 7 metrics of record — so it cannot answer the question the gate actually asks (*"is this champion better than that one?"*). **That leaves option 3, and it is a conclusion rather than a concession:** the sealed test set was always scaffolding for a one-shot evaluation, not a renewable resource; in production the holdout is defined temporally and renews itself; this dataset has no time axis, so that mechanism is structurally unavailable. The weekly retrain is therefore exercised as **machinery** — the plumbing, gate, alias flip, and alerting are real and correct — and not as live learning. Being able to say precisely *why*, and to show it is the dataset's shape rather than a gap in the method, is worth more than a rolling holdout that merely looks live.
  >
  > This remains a **Phase 10 decision** — it does not block Phases 6–9 — but it should be **recorded as resolved in favour of option 3 before `training_cycle.py` is written**, rather than re-litigated then. `ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13 already carries the substance; the plan should simply stop describing the choice as open. Keep option 1 documented as the answer a real deployment would use, so the design's correctness is legible even though this instance cannot exercise it. The one outcome to avoid is shipping a gate that silently re-scores the spent test set every Sunday.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — does the direction sanity check recur every retrain cycle, or was it a cold-start-only gate?** Phase 7's promotion gate has two structurally different parts: four automated numeric criteria (PR-AUC, recall, Brier, calibration slope) computed in `gate.py::decide_promotion`, and one human-executed veto — the direction sanity check (does any top-ranked SHAP feature's learned effect contradict the established EDA relationship?) — exercised once, in the Phase 7 notebook's closing human-review cell. The `training_cycle.py` bullet above re-specifies the automated criteria for the comparative regime (PR-AUC improvement, recall/Brier vetoes) but never mentions the direction sanity check. Segment collapse, fairness disparity, and per-group calibration collapse get the opposite, explicit treatment two bullets down — a deliberate promotion from "one-time reported diagnostic" to "continuously-enforced," via `performance_check.py`. The direction sanity check gets no equivalent statement either way: nothing carries it forward as a recurring automated check, and nothing retires it as cold-start-only either. Two candidate resolutions, neither chosen:
  >
  > 1. **Cold-start-only, by design.** Once the frozen feature spec's top-ranked features are verified directionally sane at launch, a weekly retrain on the *same* spec is unlikely to relearn a backwards relationship absent a pipeline bug — and a pipeline bug (a stale join, a flipped sign) is exactly what the dummy-floor guardrail and PSI/drift monitoring already exist to catch, from a different angle. Requires no new code; just say so explicitly, the same way the sealed-test-erosion question above was resolved by *naming* the boundary rather than building around it.
  > 2. **Recompute automatically, page a human only on failure.** `direction_sanity_check` is already a pure function over a persisted correlation, not a subjective judgment — `training_cycle.py` could re-run it every cycle against the same `_EXPECTED_EDA_DIRECTIONS` dictionary and treat a fresh contradiction as a structured alarm (mirroring the dummy-floor guardrail above), rather than requiring a human to open a notebook every Sunday. Keeps the veto's teeth without the manual step, at the cost of a dictionary that itself needs maintaining as features change.
  >
  > Resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written, same as the two open questions above.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — does the weekly cycle re-run the Optuna search, or refit with pinned hyperparameters?** The plan has already accepted this exact reasoning one axis over: the feature-selection note above freezes the input space because a borderline feature flipping between cycles "would confound tuning and make model versions hard to compare week to week." **Hyperparameters are the adjacent axis and have received no equivalent statement either way.** The stake is the comparative gate itself: scheduled retraining is supposed to answer *"did new data help?"*, and a fresh search changes the data **and** the hyperparameters at once, so a challenger that wins or loses cannot be attributed. The industry split is not tooling-driven — HPO is a first-class pipeline component in Katib, SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning, and Vertex Vizier, so "in the DAG" is thoroughly standard — it is driven by *whether the optimum is expected to move*. Search and refit are two different activities that get conflated because they run the same code: search is wide, expensive and reviewed; refit is cheap, deterministic and unattended. The common resolution is to check the search result into config and re-search on a slower, separately-reviewed cadence.
  >
  > **The question is currently latent rather than live, and that is itself the hazard.** `_study_name()` is content-addressed on `data_content_hash` with `load_if_exists=True`, so an unchanged dataset resumes the existing study rather than re-searching — and DVC skips the `train` stage entirely before that even matters. The 50 trials are therefore paid exactly when the data changes, which is approximately never on this project's static CSV. So today the behaviour is right by accident: it is an emergent property of the caching key, not a stated policy, and it becomes live the moment a Phase 12 environment rebuilds from scratch or any real feed appears. Three candidate resolutions, none chosen:
  >
  > 1. **Pin for scheduled retrains; re-search only on manual trigger or a drift alarm.** The industry default, and the one that makes the comparative gate clean — one thing changes per cycle. Needs a `tuning.mode: pinned | search` knob, with the pinned values resolved from the **champion's own logged params** via the alias, never a hand-edited config block (that is the anti-pattern both camps agree on: a search done once, its result retyped into YAML, and no record of the search that produced it).
  > 2. **Re-search every cycle, warm-started from the champion** — the mechanism the warm-start note above already proposes. Keeps the DAG self-contained and the search auditable; narrows the search around the incumbent so the confounding is partial rather than total; costs a full study per data change.
  > 3. **Status quo — rely on content-addressed study memoisation.** Cheapest, and on a static dataset indistinguishable from (1). Its defect is legibility, not behaviour: nothing states the policy, so the first person to change the caching key changes the retraining semantics without knowing it.
  >
  > **Whichever is chosen, the mechanism is already constrained — the policy and its trigger live in different systems, and the pinned values cannot be fetched inside the stage.** Three parts: **(a) the policy is a param.** `tuning.mode: pinned | search` is a named key in `configs/tuning/optuna.yaml`, which Phase 8 already declares under `train`'s `params:` — so flipping it changes a hashed param and correctly invalidates `train`, for free, with no `dvc.yaml` rework. **(b) The trigger is not a param, and DVC cannot express it.** "Re-search quarterly" and "re-search when drift fires" are time- and event-based conditions; DVC's staleness detection is content-hash based and has no notion of either. Setting the mode for a given run is Prefect's job — the same DVC-owns-recomputation / Prefect-owns-scheduling line Phase 8 draws. The one exception is option 3: **"re-search iff the data changed" is exactly what content-hashing encodes**, which is why `_study_name()` produces it for free and why that option needs no orchestrator at all. **(c) In `pinned` mode the values come from the champion's logged params — a live alias read, which is forbidden inside a stage.** This is bit-for-bit the undeclared-dependency problem Phase 8 resolves for `evaluate.py`'s comparison baseline, and it takes the same resolution: `training_cycle.py` resolves the champion's params **once at flow start** and passes them into the train step as an explicit parameter. Reading the alias inside `train` is the same category error as reading it inside `evaluate`.
  >
  > Resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written. Note that (2) is not a compromise between (1) and (3) — it is a third position, and choosing it means accepting that week-to-week model comparisons carry a hyperparameter difference the gate does not model.
  > **📌 Cost data point from the Phase 8 crosscheck rerun — informative, not decisive.** A live `make train` run measured `run_tuning_step` (50 trials, 5-fold CV) at ~4 minutes, cheaper than the feature-selection ablation this project already froze (tens of minutes, ~85,000 scoring passes). Worth recording because it rules out one bad argument for option 1: pinning would not be rescuing this pipeline from an expensive search — at this data scale the search is already cheap. **This does not resolve the open question above** — the reason to consider pinning here was never cost, it is attribution (a fresh search changes data and hyperparameters at once, so the comparative gate can't tell which one a challenger's win came from), and that argument is unaffected by how many minutes the search takes. Recorded so a future reader doesn't reach for "it's cheap, so just re-search every cycle" as if that settled it.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — should "inherits the operating threshold" be unconditional, or gated on `within_ci`?** Phase 6's `threshold.py` already computes an agreement diagnostic every time it runs: `within_ci` — whether the closed-form `t*` falls inside the bootstrap CI of the empirical argmax-EV threshold. Today that check is passive — a `logger.warning("threshold_argmax_disagreement", ...)` a human reads after a manual run, nothing more (see `run_threshold_step`, `threshold.py:380-390`). As written, Phase 10's `training_cycle.py` inherits the shipped threshold **unconditionally** every cycle, on the reasoning that `t*` is a pure function of `configs/costs.yaml` and doesn't move when the model does. But `within_ci` exists precisely to catch the case where that reasoning breaks: a re-calibration cycle producing a materially different probability distribution near the operating point, such that the closed-form cut no longer agrees empirically with where expected value is actually maximised. Candidate resolutions, none yet chosen:
  >
  > 1. **Recompute `within_ci` each cycle, unconditionally inherit `t*` regardless.** Cheapest — the diagnostic becomes a monitored metric (logged, alertable) but never blocks or auto-triggers anything. Consistent with "one metric drives selection" (`CLAUDE.md`), since `t*` isn't a selection criterion, but risks the disagreement sitting unnoticed in logs for cycles at a time.
  > 2. **`within_ci == False` triggers an automatic re-derivation** (`run_threshold_step` called instead of inherited) as part of that cycle's `training_cycle.py` run. Keeps the threshold self-correcting, but blurs the "cost-driven, not model-driven" boundary the inherit-by-default design rests on, and needs a decision on whether re-derivation can silently change the shipped threshold or must itself be gated (e.g. behind the same manual sign-off as a `costs.yaml` edit).
  > 3. **`within_ci == False` blocks promotion with a structured alarm**, mirroring the dummy-floor guardrail in the bullet above — treat sustained disagreement as a signal that calibration (not the threshold) needs attention, and let a human decide whether to re-derive, re-calibrate, or investigate further.
  >
  > **Deferred deliberately**, same as the promotion-gate question above — resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written and record the choice in `ANALYSIS.md`.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — `require_review: false` for the weekly cycle was never decided on its merits; decide it here.** Phase 7 defaults `require_review: true` (`configs/register/default.yaml`) because a human stamps the verdict via the review CLI each cycle; Phase 10's weekly `training_cycle.py` sets it `false` for the no-human-in-the-loop automated cycle. Nothing in this plan states whether `false` is the permanent Phase 10+ state or whether some condition re-inserts a human.
  >
  > **The framing matters, because the obvious version of this question smuggles in an answer.** Asking *"does `false` ever flip back to `true`?"* presupposes `false` was chosen and asks only whether to carve an exception — which hands option 1 below the incumbent's advantage it never earned. `false` is better read as **residue of a constraint**: the review mechanism was Jupyter-bound, an unattended weekly flow cannot open JupyterLab, so review was switched off rather than re-mechanised. **Phase 8's review CLI (PR B1) removes that constraint**, which is precisely why the decision has to be re-derived rather than defended: a weekly human sign-off may still be the wrong call — the four gate criteria plus vetoes are doing the real work — but that has to be argued, not inherited. Same three candidates, no default:
  >
  > 1. **Never re-escalate.** `false` is permanent from Phase 10 on — the automated gate's four numeric criteria plus vetoes are what a no-human cycle is defined to trust; re-escalating on top of that would concede the automation was never really trusted.
  > 2. **Escalate on a gate-boundary or rejected outcome.** A cycle that fails the gate, or passes within some margin of a veto threshold, routes to a human review before the alias flip instead of auto-rejecting/auto-promoting — the same "alarm on ambiguity" pattern the dummy-floor and `within_ci` guardrails already use elsewhere in this phase.
  > 3. **Periodic audit sample.** Every Nth cycle (or a fixed cadence) forces `require_review: true` regardless of outcome, as a spot-check that the automated gate hasn't drifted from what a human would actually approve.
  >
  > Resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written, same as the other open questions above.
  >
  > **A fourth case sits outside all three options: cold start has no rollback target, so it should force `require_review: true` unconditionally, regardless of whichever of 1–3 governs the steady state.** Options 1–3 all implicitly assume an incumbent `champion` exists — `rollback_champion()` reverts to a version tagged `promotion_status: promoted`, and that only works if one exists. If `training_cycle.py` ever runs with **no version currently tagged `promoted`** (a wiped registry, a disaster-recovery rebuild, a fresh environment) and the automated gate alone promotes a bad candidate, there is nothing to fall back to — the usual safety net that makes auto-approval defensible in the comparative regime simply isn't there. So: `if no promoted version exists: require_review = true`, checked ahead of and overriding whichever policy 1–3 is chosen. In practice this project's actual cold start already happened manually in Phase 7, before Prefect exists, so `training_cycle.py` may never hit this path — but the rule should exist rather than leave a first-ever automated promotion silently trusting the same policy designed for a world where rollback is possible.
  >
  > **Whichever of 1–3 is chosen, the notebook cannot be the escalation mechanism — "a human stamps `review` via notebook 05" is a Phase 7 premise that stops holding the moment `training_cycle.py` runs unattended, on a schedule, with nobody positioned to have JupyterLab open.** Options 2 and 3 both *require* review to happen sometimes post-Phase-10, so whichever is picked needs a mechanism that assumes zero standing human attention until the moment escalation fires. The natural fit is **Prefect's own human-in-the-loop primitive**: the flow calls `pause_flow_run` (or a `Suspend`) at the point `register.py` would otherwise run, which persists the run's state and fires a notification (Slack/email/PagerDuty) rather than blocking a process someone has to remember is running. The notification links to the artifacts already on disk — `error_analysis.json`, `promotion_decision.json` — no notebook needed to *view* them, since they are files. A reviewer resumes the run by submitting a verdict through a typed input, which invokes the same `gate.py::record_review` `models/review.py` calls today — so this is a production-hardening of open-question candidate #2 from Phase 8 (the small CLI wrapping `record_review`), not a new mechanism: that CLI becomes the resume action Prefect calls, in place of a human running it by hand. Pre-Phase-10, manual CLI review remains the only option — this mechanism has nothing to attach to before Prefect orchestrates the cycle.
  >
  > **📌 Three separate axes are easy to conflate here — worth stating together, since the natural-sounding mistake is to assume cold start and retrain use different review paths.** They don't; only *whether* the one path fires differs.
  > 1. **DVC stage execution is regime-blind.** The nine `dvc.yaml` stages (Phase 8) run identically whether or not a `champion` currently exists — the DAG has no dependency on, and no visibility into, registry state. Cold start and a retrain-with-incumbent produce a candidate through the exact same `dvc repro` chain.
  > 2. **The promotion decision's regime-awareness lives entirely inside the automated criteria, computed in `evaluate.py`.** `gate.py::decide_promotion` evaluates cold start against absolute bars and the comparative regime against relative-improvement criteria versus the incumbent (`ANALYSIS.md` §0). This is the "automatic part" — already regime-aware today, unrelated to review.
  > 3. **The review-escalation *mechanism* is one thing, invoked under different conditions — not two different things.** The Prefect suspend → notify → resume-via-CLI path described above is identical regardless of regime. What differs by regime is only whether it fires: cold start forces `require_review = true` unconditionally (no rollback target exists yet); the comparative regime governs firing by whichever of options 1–3 above gets chosen. There is one escalation path, gated differently — not a cold-start path and a separate retrain path.
  > **📌 Surface Phase 8's `*_elbow_drifted` warnings in this flow's monitoring surface, not `model_card.json`.** `explain.check_top_k_elbow` (Phase 8 PR B2) verifies at every `threshold.py`/`error_analysis.py` run that the hand-derived top-k cutoffs (V3's `threshold.v3_top_k_features`, plus `error_analysis`'s `top_k_dependence_features`/`top_k_cohort_gap_features`/`top_k_cohort_gap_features_fp_tn`) still sit on a real elbow in the current SHAP ranking, and logs a structlog warning (`*_elbow_drifted`) if not — but it never raises, never suggests a replacement k, and nothing today routes that warning anywhere a human watches; under Phase 8's manual, one-off runs a human reading the terminal is an adequate channel, but it stops being one the moment `training_cycle.py` runs unattended on a schedule. `model_card.json` is the wrong destination for it regardless: the model card is stakeholder-facing narrative (`CLAUDE.md` — "no hyperparameters, hashes, or raw statistical detail... that lives in `training_manifest.json`"), and an elbow-ratio drift signal on an internal SHAP ranking cutoff is exactly that kind of internal diagnostic, not model-quality narrative a stakeholder needs. Route it instead through this flow's own structured-alarm channel — the same Prefect-notification pattern already used for the dummy-floor guardrail and the `within_ci` disagreement above — or fold it into the weekly cycle's summary alongside those. The response to a fired warning stays manual (re-derive the cutoff by hand from the current ranking, the same process as the original Phase 8 derivation) — this only closes the gap between "the check already exists" and "a human operating the unattended cycle will actually see it."
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — does the weekly cycle still run `register.py` to completion on a week where nothing changed?** `customers_raw` is static and non-growing (the clarification above already establishes `ingest`/`validate`/`features` as cache hits every cycle), and most weeks neither code nor `configs/` will have changed either. As `training_cycle.py` is currently specified, the flow still runs the *entire* chain — `train` (resuming the memoised Optuna study, so this part is cheap per the cost data point below) `→ calibrate → evaluate → register` — unconditionally every Sunday. Because the model is bit-identical to the current champion on a no-op week (same `data_content_hash`, same resumed study, same selected trial, `random_state=42` throughout), `evaluate.py`'s comparative-regime gate resolves exactly the way it did on this project's own no-op cycle earlier in Phase 8's manual testing: `pr_auc`'s `delta_obs = 0.0` against the incumbent, failing the materiality threshold regardless of the absolute bar — a predictable `rejected` outcome, known before the cycle runs. **The cost is not wasted compute — `train`'s resumed study is cheap — it is a permanent audit-trail one.** `CLAUDE.md`'s registry-hygiene policy keeps every `rejected` version unconditionally (only stale `pending` orphans are reaped), so a `rejected`-by-construction version minted 52 times a year, forever, is not clutter the reaper ever touches — it is unbounded, by-design growth in the version list a human scans under pressure during an emergency rollback, the exact hazard `CLAUDE.md`'s tag-based rollback rule exists to keep legible. Three candidate resolutions, none yet chosen:
  >
  > 1. **Skip the cycle before `train`, on a receipt/hash check.** Compare the current `data_content_hash`/`training_manifest.json` inputs against the champion's own logged manifest before starting; an unchanged fingerprint short-circuits the flow with a `cycle_skipped_no_change` structured event instead of running anything. Cheapest in registry terms, but needs the comparison built and run *before* `train`, not after — the receipt-diff check alone (option 2) can't prevent the `train`/`calibrate` cost, only the `register` one.
  > 2. **Run through `calibrate` unconditionally (it's cheap), gate `register.py` on whether `calibrate`'s `run_id` actually changed.** The same mechanism this project's own local/CI workflow already uses: `calibrate_receipt.json`'s `run_id` is unchanged iff DVC-equivalent caching found nothing to redo, and a genuinely new `run_id` is definitive proof something was actually different this cycle. Lets `train`'s cheap study-resume run every week as a live health check (a completed-vs-broken pipeline signal) while sparing the registry the predictable-`rejected` outcome.
  > 3. **Status quo — register unconditionally every cycle.** Cheapest to build, and arguably an honest demonstration that the automation ran even on a no-op week. Its cost is exactly the unbounded audit-trail growth above, and it should be chosen deliberately if at all, not by default from never having asked the question.
  >
  > Resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written, same as the other open questions in this section.
  > **❓ OPEN QUESTION — under candidate 2 above, what model version does `evaluate.py` resolve against on a `costs.yaml`-only change, where `calibrate` stays cached but `threshold`/`evaluate`/`error_analysis` correctly rerun?** Surfaced while sketching a CI equivalent of candidate 2 for Phase 11: gating `register.py` on whether `calibrate_receipt.json`'s `run_id` moved is the right condition for *should a new version be minted*, and DVC's own dependency graph already reruns `threshold → evaluate → error_analysis` correctly on a `costs.yaml`-only edit independent of that condition (confirmed empirically during Phase 8's own DAG-invalidation testing — editing `costs.yaml` alone reran exactly that three-stage chain, `calibrate` untouched). But those two facts collide: `costs.yaml` changes recompute `t*` (`threshold.py`), which changes the recall gate criterion `evaluate.py` computes at the new operating point — a genuine, gate-relevant recomputation — yet no new model version exists to run it against, because `calibrate`'s unchanged output means candidate 2's own condition correctly declines to mint one. `evaluate.py` "resolves its model by explicit `run_id` or version number, never by alias" (`CLAUDE.md`), so it needs *some* already-registered version to target, and the two candidates are both awkward: re-target the current `champion`/`challenger` version's existing registration (re-evaluating an already-`promoted` version against a new threshold has no clean precedent in the promote/reject vocabulary this project has built), or skip the rerun entirely (silently leaving the shipped `t*` stale relative to the current `costs.yaml` — close in spirit to the `within_ci` disagreement two open questions above, but for the threshold's *inputs* rather than its *empirical agreement*). Not resolved here — flagged as a gap in candidate 2's own mechanism, to settle alongside the other Phase 10 open questions before `training_cycle.py` (or its Phase 11 CI analogue) is written.
- `pipelines/drift_check.py` — daily 06:00; pulls the last 24 hours of predictions from the API structured logs; runs an Evidently data drift report **against `drift_reference.json` fetched from the currently-`champion`-aliased run** (Phase 7 `register.py`) — never against a fixed path or a re-derived baseline, so a rollback automatically restores the matching reference; alerts (Prefect notification) when PSI > 0.2 on any top-5 feature. **Prevalence is monitored alongside feature PSI and routes differently:** a sustained prevalence shift is a *re-calibration* trigger, not a retrain trigger (§9 limitation #12 — they are different remedies).
  - **⚠ Open detail: what makes a prevalence shift "sustained" is not yet specified.** Feature drift has a concrete number (PSI > 0.2); prevalence does not — the routing rule above names the concept but not the test. Prevalence is a single proportion, not a multi-bin distribution, so a PSI-style magnitude heuristic isn't the natural fit here the way it is for features; a two-proportion comparison (a confidence interval on the current-window churn rate against `drift_reference.json`'s stored prevalence, required to persist across multiple measurement windows rather than firing on one noisy reading) is a reasonable candidate when this module is actually built, but it is not yet a committed design.
  - **⚠ Open gap: no recalibration pipeline exists that is actually distinct from a full retrain.** The rule above says a prevalence shift should trigger recalibration rather than retraining, but nowhere in this plan is there a `recalibrate.py`-style flow analogous to `training_cycle.py` or the `rollback_champion()` helper — the only place `calibrate.py` runs at all is as one step inside `training_cycle.py`'s full cycle (`train → calibrate → evaluate → register`). As specified today, "recalibrate" and "retrain" resolve to the same pipeline, which defeats the reason for naming them separate remedies in the first place — recalibration is supposed to be the cheaper, faster fix, not a relabelled full cycle. Closing this gap needs a lightweight flow that reuses `calibrate.py` against the frozen, already-trained champion spec (no `train.py` refit, no feature or hyperparameter changes) and re-derives the threshold's contact-rate figure against the new calibration map — but that flow is not yet designed.
- `pipelines/performance_check.py` — **realized-performance feedback loop (closes the ML loop — `input` drift is only a proxy for what we actually care about).** PSI and prediction-distribution drift detect that *inputs* moved; they do not measure whether the model is still *right*. For churn the ground truth does arrive — observed weeks/months later — so this flow joins **logged predictions → realised outcomes** once labels mature and computes **realised PR-AUC** (plus Brier and the decile-lift table) over the matured cohort — **both aggregate and sliced by the same Phase 7 robustness axes** (`contract_type`, `tenure_cohort`, `internetservice`), so segment-concentrated decay surfaces before it drags the aggregate down (the production analogue of Phase 7's V1 segment-collapse diagnostic — the continuous guard against subgroup collapse that a single sealed-test snapshot could never power) — comparing each to the Phase 7 offline estimate. **This is also where V2/V2b's enforcement actually lives**: the same matured cohorts are sliced by the **fairness axes** (`gender`, `seniorcitizen`, `has_partner`, `dependents`), computing realised equal-opportunity and demographic-parity gaps at the shipped `t*` (V2) and realised per-group calibration slope (V2b), each compared to its Phase 7 test-set baseline using the same segment-CI-derived alert-band approach as the robustness axes below — turning V1/V2/V2b from a one-time reported diagnostic (`ANALYSIS.md` §0) into a continuously-enforced one, once production volume supplies the power a single snapshot can't. **The baseline is Phase 7's *test-set* slice metrics, not its dev-OOF ones** — Phase 7 produces both, and they serve different purposes: the dev-OOF slices are non-gating diagnostics reported at promotion time (chosen for statistical power), while the test-set slices are the published metrics of record and what the model card told stakeholders. Production monitoring compares against what was published, so one number means one thing. **The thin-support caveat propagates:** a segment whose test baseline carries a wide CI (the two-year `contract_type` tier, ~10 churners) cannot support a tight degradation alert — derive that segment's alert band from its baseline CI rather than a fixed global threshold, or it will page on noise indefinitely. Mechanics: predictions are logged with `customerid` + `request_id` + timestamp (Phase 9); a `prediction_outcomes` table in Postgres records the eventual churn label per `customerid`; the flow runs on a **label-maturity cadence** (e.g. monthly, after the observation window closes), scoring only the cohort whose outcomes are now known. **Delayed-label discipline:** never score a cohort before its outcome window matures (premature labels bias the metric optimistic); the maturity lag is a configured constant. This realised number — not PSI — is the **authoritative health signal** and the primary, *performance-based* retrain trigger (see below).
  > **📌 `prediction_outcomes` is this project's first non-regenerable table, which makes this the phase that adopts Alembic — moved here from Phase 8, and deliberately *not* deferred to Phase 12's RDS.** Every table before it is a materialised cache of the static raw CSV, recoverable by `dvc repro --force`; this one accrues matured labels over months and nothing rebuilds it. That is exactly the criterion migrations exist for — *state you cannot regenerate* — and it is met three phases before RDS, on local Docker Postgres, with no AWS involved. Adopting it here also means there is a real second schema version to record rather than a speculative one: `sql/schema/001_create_raw.sql` becomes migration `001`, `prediction_outcomes` becomes `002`. See the Phase 8 note for the full argument, including why adopting at RDS time would diverge local from production. **`ingest.py` must stop executing the raw DDL directly** in the same change — `alembic upgrade head` becomes the single source of schema truth — and the whole thing lands as a standalone `chore/` PR just before this phase needs it.
